## Should you add more NeurIPS citations to reduce desk-rejection risk?

**No — this isn't how NeurIPS desk-rejection works.** Desk rejects are triggered by formatting/length violations, anonymity breaks, dual-submission, or out-of-scope topic. There is no policy or reviewer norm rewarding venue self-citation, and reviewers can spot citation padding. The official 2026 reviewer guidelines and checklist make no mention of citation-venue distribution.

What your current distribution actually signals is healthy: top-tier ML venues (NeurIPS+ICML+ICLR) make up **48%** of citations, which is normal for a TSFM/MoE paper. The 27% arXiv share is also defensible because many cited works are 2025–2026 (TimeMoE, TTM, Chronos, Time-MoE, ERMoE, RF-MoE, IB-Norm, Time-R1, etc.) — these are genuinely arXiv-only at submission time.

The real risks I'd worry about instead:
1. **Page-9 limit** (your `todo.md` flags main body slightly over).
2. **Bibliography integrity** — `bib_verification.xlsx` should cover all 62 entries; verify newly added 2026 arXiv refs (liu2026routingfree, liu2026timers1, berthelier2026revin, wang2026myth) actually exist with the listed IDs, since fabricated citations are a real desk-reject risk.
3. **Anonymity** — confirm no `\thanks{}` or non-anonymized links slipped in.

If a *specific* relevant NeurIPS paper is missing (e.g., MoE routing-collapse work, RevIN follow-ups, mutual-information bounds), add it on merit — but don't pad. Want me to audit for missing relevant NeurIPS papers in your specific subareas (MoE collapse, TSFM adaptation, MI bounds)?

Based on the search results, here are some relevant findings related to time series foundation models, adaptation, and fine-tuning at NeurIPS 2024-2025:

## Time Series Foundation Model Research

NeurIPS 2024 hosted a workshop called "Time Series in the Age of Large Models" that delved into building foundation models for time series, leveraging pretrained models of other modalities for time series, and multimodal time series models.

## Recent Work on Adaptation and Fine-tuning

A NeurIPS 2025 workshop paper proposes a dynamic two-stream framework that augments pre-trained time series foundation models with frequency pattern awareness and contextual retrieval. This work was presented as a poster in the "Recent Advances in Time Series Foundation Models: Have We Reached the 'BERT Moment'?" workshop on December 7, 2025.

## Toolkit for TSFM Pipelines

FMTK, an open-source toolkit introduced at NeurIPS 2025, enables construction of time series foundation model pipelines via standardized backbone and component abstractions. Recent pipelines combine TSFMs with task-specific encoders, decoders, and adapters to improve performance.

## Parameter-Efficient Methods

Parameter-efficient adaptation methods like adapters and LoRA remain common approaches across recent NeurIPS papers (2023-2025).

The search results reveal significant activity in time series foundation model research at NeurIPS, with particular emphasis on adaptation techniques, modularity, and practical applications.

I've found relevant research related to Mixture of Experts (MoE), routing, and load balancing presented at NeurIPS 2024 and related venues. Here's what the search revealed:

## Key Research Areas

### NeurIPS 2024 Papers

**"Toward Efficient Inference for Mixture of Experts"** is a prominent NeurIPS 2024 paper addressing MoE deployment challenges. The paper identifies that deploying MoE models for inference is difficult due to their large model size and complex communication patterns, and proposes three optimization techniques: dynamic gating, expert buffering, and expert load balancing. The dynamic gating approach improves maximum throughput by 6.21-11.55× for language modeling, 5.75-10.98× for machine translation encoding, and 2.58-5.71× for machine translation decoding.

### Routing Collapse and Load Balancing

For Mixture-of-Experts models, an unbalanced expert load will lead to routing collapse or increased computational overhead. While existing methods commonly employ an auxiliary loss to encourage load balance, a large auxiliary loss will introduce interference gradients into training, so researchers have proposed Loss-Free Balancing, a new load balancing strategy that operates without auxiliary losses.

### Multi-Head MoE and Advanced Architectures

Multi-Head MoE (MH-MoE) maintains strengths including higher expert activation and better scalability, and can alleviate lower expert activation problems while enhancing usage of larger expert counts.

These papers collectively address core challenges in scaling MoE models, particularly around preventing routing collapse and maintaining efficient expert utilization during both training and inference.

Based on the search results, I found several relevant papers from NeurIPS 2024 and 2025 related to mutual information bounds, variational inference, and tractable methods:

## NeurIPS 2024 Papers

1. **Mutual Information Estimation via Normalizing Flows** - This work obtains a cheap and tractable lower bound on the KL divergence via an entropy-cross-entropy decomposition. The method uses diffeomorphisms to facilitate mutual information estimation, achieving tractable MI in the latent space, where MI between latent representations equals MI between the original random variables.

2. **VISA: Variational Inference with Sequential Sample-Average Approximations** - This work approximates an intractable target density with a tractable variational distribution by solving an optimization problem.

3. **Mutual Information Estimation via f-Divergence and Data Derangements** - The paper proposes a family of MI estimators based on the variational lower bound of the f-divergence and argues that maximization of any f-divergence VLB can lead to an MI estimator with excellent bias/variance trade-off.

## NeurIPS 2025 Paper

4. **Fisher meets Feynman: score-based variational inference with a product of experts** - This work introduces a highly expressive yet distinctly tractable family for black-box variational inference where each member is a weighted product of experts proportional to a multivariate t-distribution.

These papers address various aspects of tractable variational bounds and mutual information estimation relevant to your search query.

The search results show significant research on sparse mixture of experts, particularly addressing the representation collapse problem. Here's what I found:

## Key Research on Representation Collapse in Sparse Mixture of Experts

Sparse mixture of experts provides larger model capacity while requiring a constant computational overhead, employing a routing mechanism to distribute input tokens to the best-matched experts according to their hidden representations. However, learning such a routing mechanism encourages token clustering around expert centroids, implying a trend toward representation collapse.

### Addressing the Problem

One approach proposes to estimate the routing scores between tokens and experts on a low-dimensional hypersphere. More specifically, the method involves projecting the hidden vectors into a lower-dimensional space, applying L2 normalization to both token representations and expert embeddings to measure routing scores on a low-dimensional hypersphere, and proposing a soft expert gate with learnable temperature to control expert activation.

### Results

Experimental results across seven multilingual benchmarks show consistent gains, with comprehensive analysis on representation and routing behaviors demonstrating that the method alleviates the representation collapse issue and achieves more consistent routing than baseline mixture-of-experts methods.

**Note:** The search returned results primarily from NeurIPS 2022, rather than NeurIPS 2025. The most prominent paper on this topic was presented at the 36th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2022).

## Key Findings:

**NeurIPS 2025 LTSF Research:**

NeurIPS 2025 was held in two locations - at the Hilton Mexico City Reforma in Mexico City from Sunday, November 30, through Friday, December 5, 2025, and at the San Diego Convention Center in San Diego from Tuesday, December 2, through Sunday, December 7, 2025.

**Notable LTSF Papers at NeurIPS 2025:**

1. **AliO (Output Alignment):** Long-term Time Series Forecasting (LTSF) tasks leverage the current data sequence as input to predict the future sequence and have become increasingly crucial in real-world applications such as weather forecasting and planning of electricity consumption. AliO is a novel approach designed to improve the output alignment of LTSF models by reducing the discrepancies between prediction outputs for the same timestamps in both the time and frequency domains.

2. **LTSM-Bundle:** A toolbox and benchmark on Large Language Models for Time Series Forecasting, where researchers are developing Large Time Series Models (LTSMs), universal transformer-based models that use autoregressive prediction to improve TSF.

3. **MoFo:** MoFo achieves competitive performance while maintaining high memory efficiency and fast training speed on widely used benchmark datasets.

**Benchmark Evaluation Perspectives:**

Informer standardized evaluation protocols on widely adopted benchmarks such as Electricity Transformer (ETT) and Weather, evaluating models on fixed benchmark datasets under predefined forecasting horizons (e.g., 96, 192, 336, and 720 time steps) using pointwise error metrics including mean squared error (MSE) and mean absolute error (MAE).

Based on the search results, here's what I found about RevIN and related work on instance normalization for time series forecasting:

## RevIN (Reversible Instance Normalization)

RevIN is a generally-applicable normalization-and-denormalization method with learnable affine transformation designed to address distribution shift in time series forecasting. Statistical properties such as mean and variance often change over time in time series, i.e., time-series data suffer from a distribution shift problem, and this change in temporal distribution is one of the main challenges that prevent accurate time-series forecasting.

RevIN calculates the mean and standard deviation of each feature separately for each sequence in a mini-batch. RevIN is a flexible, end-to-end trainable layer that can be applied to any arbitrarily chosen layers, effectively suppressing non-stationary information (mean and variance of the instance) in one layer and restoring it in another layer at a virtually symmetric position, e.g., input and output layers.

## NeurIPS 2024 Related Work

The search results show that NeurIPS 2024 featured related work building on RevIN. Ye et al. (2024) introduced Frequency Adaptive Normalization for non-stationary time series forecasting, published in Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems, 37, 2024. This work represents an advancement beyond RevIN's approach, as Ye et al. (2024) normalize in the whole frequency domain, which is a promising direction for improving upon RevIN's limitations.

Additionally, there's recent critical analysis of RevIN: RevIN does not address all forms of heterogeneity in distribution shift problems.

Based on the search results, here are the key findings related to MoE expert utilization and auxiliary-loss-free balancing approaches:

## Loss-Free Balancing Method

Loss-Free Balancing is a novel MoE load balancing method that dynamically adjusts expert biases based on recent load without relying on auxiliary losses, thereby avoiding interference gradients and achieving improved model performance. Before the top-K routing decision, Loss-Free Balancing applies an expert-wise bias to the routing scores of each expert, and by dynamically updating the bias of each expert according to its recent load, Loss-Free Balancing can consistently maintain a balanced distribution of expert load.

## Problem with Traditional Auxiliary Loss Approaches

For Mixture-of-Experts models, an unbalanced expert load will lead to routing collapse or increased computational overhead, and existing methods commonly employ an auxiliary loss to encourage load balance, but a large auxiliary loss will introduce non-negligible interference gradients into training and thus impair model performance.

## NeurIPS 2025 Theoretical Framework

A theoretical framework was provided for analyzing the Auxiliary-Loss-Free Load Balancing (ALF-LB) procedure --- proposed by DeepSeek's Wang et al. (2024) --- by casting it as a one-step-per-iteration primal-dual method for an assignment problem. The framework yields several insightful structural properties: (i) a monotonic improvement of a Lagrangian objective, (ii) a preference rule that moves tokens from overloaded to underloaded experts, and (iii) an approximate-balancing guarantee.

## Experimental Results

Loss-Free Balancing was validated on MoE models with up to 3B parameters trained on up to 200B tokens, and experimental results show that Loss-Free Balancing achieves both better performance and better load balance compared with traditional auxiliary-loss-controlled load balancing strategies.

The search returned several relevant results from NeurIPS 2025 related to foundation models for time series. Here are the key findings:

## Workshop Overview

The NeurIPS 2025 workshop on "Recent Advances in Time Series Foundation Models: Have We Reached the BERT Moment?" was held at the San Diego Convention Center on December 7, 2025.

## Key Research Themes

**Foundation Model Capabilities and Transfer Learning:**
The inherent diversity of time series data makes foundation models less suited for building effective foundation models compared to language and vision models. The zero-shot capabilities of a time series foundation model are significantly influenced and tied to the specific domains it has been pretrained on.

**Probing and Interpretability:**
Researchers conducted a systematic investigation of concept interpretability in TSFMs by examining which layers encode which concepts, whether concept parameters are linearly recoverable, how representations evolve across model depth, and how models process compositions of concepts using layer-wise analyses, linear recoverability tests, and representation similarity measures. Early layers mainly capture local, time-domain patterns while deeper layers encode dispersion and change-time signals, but probe performance degrades in compositional settings, revealing interference between concepts.

**LTSF (Long-Term Series Forecasting):**
Time-series forecasting faces challenges from non-stationary and multivariate data, and state-of-the-art TSFMs rely on fixed-size windows that miss transient phenomena.

I found several relevant results on mixture of experts for time series forecasting from NeurIPS 2024-2025. Here's a summary of the key papers:

## NeurIPS 2024 Papers

**MOIRAI-MOE**: This paper proposes MOIRAI-MOE, which improves upon the MOIRAI time series foundation model by using a single projection layer and delegating the modeling of diverse time series patterns through token-level specialization with experts, achieving superior unified learning capabilities and significant improvements in both in-distribution and zero-shot assessments.

**Time-MoE**: Introduces Time-MoE, a scalable and unified architecture for pre-training time series forecasting foundation models that leverages a sparse mixture-of-experts design to enhance computational efficiency by activating only a subset of networks for each prediction while maintaining high model capacity. The models were pre-trained on large-scale data spanning over 9 domains with over 117 billion time points, scaling up to 2.4 billion parameters and achieving significantly improved forecasting precision.

## 2025 Papers

**Time-MoE** (ICLR 2025 Spotlight): Time-MoE has been accepted by ICLR 2025 as a Spotlight presentation (Top 5.1%).

**Time Tracker**: This paper leverages sparse mixture of experts within Transformers to handle the modeling of diverse time series patterns, thereby alleviating the learning difficulties of a single model while improving its generalization.

**Wavelet Mixture of Experts**: Proposes a channel clustering strategy that incorporates a Mixture of Experts framework with a gating mechanism and expert networks to handle multi-channel dependencies efficiently in time series forecasting.

Based on the search results, here's information about NeurIPS 2024 work related to information bottleneck, representation learning, and compression theory:

## Information Bottleneck Theory Applications

Information Bottleneck theory was suggested by Tishby and Zaslavsky to quantify the trade-off in model selection, claiming that deep neural networks learn a compressed bottleneck representation that preserves only relevant information about the target label from the source data.

Constraining information input to disentangled representation learning models enables them to identify and learn the most representative factors for successful reconstruction, following Information Bottleneck theory.

## Specific NeurIPS 2024 Papers

**Protecting LLMs with Information Bottleneck:** The IBProtector defense mechanism is grounded in the information bottleneck principle and selectively compresses and perturbs prompts, preserving only essential information for target LLMs to respond with expected answers.

**Learning to Compress:** Networks that reduce the rank of their learned representations also compress mutual information between inputs and intermediate layers.

**Latent Compression Learning:** A vision model pre-training method called Latent Compression Learning performs latent compression learning by maximizing the mutual information between inputs and outputs of a causal attention model.

## NeurIPS 2024 Workshop on Machine Learning and Compression

A workshop aimed to unite researchers from machine learning, compression, and information theory, focusing on enhancing compression techniques, exploring theoretical limits, and integrating information-theoretic principles to improve learning and generalization.

I've performed a web search for "NeurIPS 2024 mixture experts router specialization spectral routing". The search returned several relevant papers from NeurIPS 2024 related to mixture-of-experts architectures and routing mechanisms:

## Key Papers Found:

1. **Read-ME: Refactorizing LLMs as Router-Decoupled Mixture of Experts** - This work refactorizes pre-trained dense LLMs into specialized experts through activation sparsity and optimizes the routing policy, with examination of the redundancy of layer-wise routers and proposal to decouple the router.

2. **Multilinear Mixture of Experts (μMoE)** - A Multilinear Mixture of Experts (μMoE) layer is proposed to address computational cost of scaling the number of experts, enabling scalable expert specialization by performing implicit computation on large weight tensors entirely in factorized form.

3. **Mixture of Nested Experts (MoNE)** - Focuses on adaptive processing of visual tokens with novel routing approaches.

4. **FuseMoE: Mixture-of-Experts Transformers for Fleximodal Fusion** - Features an MoE Fusion Layer where a routing mechanism categorizes multimodal inputs and directs them to appropriate expert combinations by evaluating similarity between input tokens and experts.

5. **MoME: Mixture of Multimodal Experts** - Exhibits relatively clear specialization of experts in different groups of tasks.

The papers generally address themes of expert specialization, routing mechanisms, and efficient activation of experts. However, the search results don't show a specific paper explicitly focused on "spectral routing" - you may want to search more specifically if that's a particular technique you're interested in learning about.

Based on the search results, I found relevant information about the NeurIPS 2024 paper on Frequency Adaptive Normalization for non-stationary time series forecasting by Ye and colleagues.

## Overview

The paper proposes Frequency Adaptive Normalization (FAN), a new instance normalization solution that extends instance normalization in handling both dynamic trend and seasonal patterns.

## Problem Statement

Time series forecasting typically needs to address non-stationary data with evolving trend and seasonal patterns. While reversible instance normalization has been proposed to alleviate impacts from the trend using statistical measures like mean and variance, they are limited to expressing basic trends and incapable of handling seasonal patterns.

## Methodology

The paper employs the Fourier transform to identify instance-wise predominant frequent components that cover most non-stationary factors. FAN then uses these identified frequencies to normalize the data, modeling the discrepancy between input and output frequencies via a simple MLP.

## Performance

Experiments across eight benchmark datasets show substantial improvements (7.76%~37.90% average improvements in MSE), highlighting FAN's potential for broader applicability and improved forecasting accuracy.

## Key Contributions

The method offers a model-agnostic solution applicable to various forecasting backbones, opening new avenues for researchers to enhance the accuracy of their models, especially when dealing with complex, real-world data exhibiting dynamic trends and seasonal patterns.

      "text": "NeurIPS A Theoretical Framework for Auxiliary-Loss-Free Load-Balancing of Sparse Mixture-of-Experts in Large-Scale AI Models NeurIPS 2025 CSP Test --\n\n- Mexico City\n- San Diego\n\nPoster in Workshop: ML x OR: Mathematical Foundations and Operational Integration of Machine Learning for Uncertainty-Aware Decision-Making\n\n# A Theoretical Framework for Auxiliary-Loss-Free Load-Balancing of Sparse Mixture-of-Experts in Large-Scale AI Models\n\nX.Y. Han · Yuan Zhong\n\nProject Page [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nIn large-scale AI training, Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (s-MoE) layers enable scaling by activating only a small subset of experts per token. An operational challenge in this design is load-balancing: routing tokens to minimize the number of idle experts, which is important for the efficient utilization of (costly) GPUs. We provide a theoretical framework for analyzing the Auxiliary-Loss-Free Load Balancing (ALF-LB) procedure --- proposed by DeepSeek's Wang et al. (2024) --- by casting it as a one-step-per-iteration primal-dual method for an assignment problem. First, in a stylized deterministic setting, our framework yields several insightful structural properties: (i) a monotonic improvement of a Lagrangian objective, (ii) a preference rule that moves tokens from overloaded to underloaded experts, and (iii) an approximate-balancing guarantee. Then, we incorporate the stochastic and dynamic nature of AI training using a generalized online optimization formulation. In the online setting, we derive a strong convexity property of the objective that leads to a logarithmic regret bound under certain step-size choices. Additionally, we present real experiments on 1B-parameter DeepSeekMoE models to complement our theoretical findings. Together, these results build a principled framework for analyzing the auxiliary-loss-free load-balancing of s-MoE in AI models.\n\nShow more\n\nChat is not available."

Existing methods contain the Loss-Controlled method and the Loss-Free method, where both the unbalanced degrees at first several training steps are still high and decrease slowly. In this work, we propose BIP-Based Balancing, an expert load balancing algorithm based on binary integer programming (BIP). The algorithm maintains an additional vector 𝒒𝒒\\bm{q}bold_italic_q on each MoE layer that can help change the top-K order of 𝒔𝒔\\bm{s}bold_italic_s by solving a binary integer programming with very small time costs. Within our current knowledge, this is the first routing algorithm that achieves maintaining load balance status on every expert in every MoE layer from the first step to the last step during the whole pre-training process, while the trained MoE models also perform well. The code material of this work is available at https://github.com/sunyuanLLM/bip_routing_algorithm.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nMoE (Mixture-of-Experts) architectures allow LLMs (Large Language Models) become sparsity so that they can have both large scale of parameters and much small resource costs. The other way is auxiliary-loss-free load balancing strategy [Wang et al., 2024], where authors add an additional bias vector on routing scores to change their sort order, instead of computing auxiliary loss.       "text": "Toward Efficient Inference for Mixture of Experts\n\n## Abstract\n\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have recently gained steam in achieving the state-of-the-art performance in a wide range of tasks in computer vision and natural language processing. However, deploying such models for inference is difficult due to their large model size and complex communication pattern. In this work, we provide a characterization of two MoE workloads, namely Language Modeling (LM) and Machine Translation (MT) and identify their sources of inefficiencies at deployment. We propose three optimization techniques to mitigate sources of inefficiencies, namely (1) Dynamic gating, (2) Expert Buffering, and (3) Expert load balancing. We further propose Expert Buffering, a new caching mechanism that only keeps hot, active experts in GPU memory while buffering the rest in CPU memory. This reduces static memory allocation by 1.47$\\times$. Finally, we propose a load balancing methodology that provides additional robustness to the workload. A learned router computes a distribution over these\nexperts, and assigns input tokens to a small subset. However, without auxiliary\nbalancing mechanisms, routers often converge to using only a few experts, severely\nlimiting model capacity and degrading performance. Most current load balancing\nmechanisms encourage a distribution over experts that resembles a roughly uniform\ndistribution of experts per token. Our experimental results show that applying our loss\nto the router results in 36% faster convergence and lower redundancy compared to\na popular load balancing loss.\n1 Introduction\nAs the demand for larger and more capable neural networks continues to grow [Kaplan et al., 2020,\nBrown et al., 2020], the need for architectures that can scale efficiently—without incurring prohibitive\ncomputational costs—has become increasingly important. This is especially true in the context\nof large language models (LLMs), where state-of-the-art performance often requires billions of\nparameters and massive training datasets. One such approach, the Mixture of Experts (MoE) model\n[Shazeer et al., 2017], introduces sparsely activated sub-networks at certain layers, allowing for\nincreased model capacity while preserving computational efficiency.\nWhile MoE architectures offer improved parameter scalability, they often suffer from poor expert\nutilization during pretraining. To encourage\nbalanced expert usage, various strategies have been proposed, including sequence-level auxiliary\nlosses such as load balancing loss (LBL) [Fedus et al., 2022] or the Expert Choice (EC) routing\nvariant which generates a distribution over a sparse set of activated tokens for each expert [Zhou et al.,\n2022].       "text": "Toward Efficient Inference for Mixture of Experts\n\nHaiyang Huang 1∗ Newsha Ardalani 2 Anna Sun 2 Liu Ke 3∗ Hsien-Hsin S. Lee 4∗ Shruti Bhosale 2 Carole-Jean Wu 2 Benjamin Lee 5∗ 1Duke University 2FAIR at Meta 3Washington University in St. Louis 4Intel Corporation 5 University of Pennsylvania hyhuang@cs.duke.edu {new, annaysun, shru, carolejeanwu}@meta.com ke.l@wustl.edu lee.sean@gmail.com leebcc@seas.upenn.edu\n\nAbstract Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have recently gained steam in achieving the state-of-the-art performance in a wide range of tasks in computer vision and natural language processing. They effectively expand the model capacity while incurring a minimal increase in computation cost during training. However, deploying such models for inference is difficult due to their large model size and complex communication pattern. In this work, we provide a characterization of two MoE workloads, namely Language Modeling (LM) and Machine Translation (MT) and identify their sources of inefficiencies at deployment. We propose three optimization techniques to mitigate sources of inefficiencies, namely (1) Dynamic gating, (2) Expert Buffering, and (3) Expert load balancing. We show that dynamic gating improves maximum throughput by 6.21-11.55× for LM, 5.75-10.98× for MT Encoder and 2.58-5.71× for MT Decoder. It also reduces memory usage by up to 1.36× for LM and up to 1.1× for MT. We further propose Expert Buffering, a new caching mechanism that only keeps hot, active experts in GPU memory while buffering the rest in CPU memory. This reduces static memory allocation by 1.47× . Finally, we propose a load balancing methodology that provides additional robustness to the workload. Our code is available at https://github.com/ hyhuang00/moe_inference.\n\n1 Introduction \nA machine learning model’s predictive ability increases with the number of parameters. Model \ncapacity has grown at an exponential rate of 10× per year [1], which in turn has driven demand for \ncomputation. Mixture of Experts (MoEs) decouple model capacity from computational demands by \nusing conditionally, sparsely activated neural networks. They can reduce training costs yet improve \naccuracy [2–4] for language modeling [5–8], machine translation [9], and image recognition [10, 11]. \nBut training is only half the story. MoE inference is important yet challenging as large language \nmodels are deployed for production services. Our experiments show MoE inference is relatively \ninefficient, requiring much more time to perform the same number of calculations. In Section 3, we \nshow that MoEs are 15× slower for language models and 3× slower for machine translation compared \nto their FLOP-equivalent dense counterparts. Distillation could shrink models and reduce latency but \nharm model quality [6, 8, 2]. Optimizations could increase parallelism and GPU utilization, but they \nnarrowly target specific kernels for communication collectives and GPU computation [12, 13]. They \n∗Work done while internin"

      "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03293",
      "title": "[2510.03293] From Score Distributions to Balance: Plug-and-Play Mixture-of-Experts Routing",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03293",
      "text": "[2510.03293] From Score Distributions to Balance: Plug-and-Play Mixture-of-Experts Routing\n\n# Computer Science > Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2510.03293 (cs)\n\n[Submitted on 29 Sep 2025]\n\n# Title:From Score Distributions to Balance: Plug-and-Play Mixture-of-Experts Routing\n\nView PDF HTML (experimental)\n\n> Abstract:Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models can scale parameter capacity by routing each token to a subset of experts through a learned gate function. While conditional routing reduces training costs, it shifts the burden on inference memory: expert parameters and activations consume memory, limiting the number of experts per device. As tokens are routed, some experts become overloaded while others are underutilized. Because experts are mapped to GPUs, this imbalance translates directly into degraded system performance in terms of latency, throughput, and cost. We present LASER, a plug-and-play, inference-time routing algorithm that balances load while preserving accuracy. LASER adapts to the shape of the gate's score distribution. When scores provide a clear preference, it routes to the strongest experts; when scores are more uniform, it broadens the set of viable experts and routes to the least-loaded among them. Because LASER relies only on gate scores from a trained model, it integrates directly into existing MoE inference pipelines without retraining or finetuning. We evaluate LASER on Mixtral-8x7B and DeepSeek-MoE-16b-chat across four datasets (ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge, MMLU, and GSM8K). LASER improves load balancing, translating into lower latency and higher throughput, while keeping the accuracy changes negligible.\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (cs.LG); Artificial Intelligence (cs.AI); Distributed, Parallel, and Cluster Computing (cs.DC) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Cite as: | arXiv:2510.03293 [cs.LG] |\n| (or arXiv:2510.03293v1 [cs.LG] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Rana Shahout [view email] [v1] Mon, 29 Sep 2025 16:29:17 UTC (4,344 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\ncs.LG\n\n< prev| next >\n\nChange to browse by:\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- Google Scholar\n- Semantic Scholar\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer (What is the Explorer?)\n\nConnected Papers Toggle\n\nConnected Papers (What is Connected Papers?)\n\nLitmaps Toggle\n\nLitmaps (What is Litmaps?)\n\nscite.ai Toggle\n\nscite Smart Citations (What are Smart Citations?)\n\nCode, Data, Media\n\n# Code, Data and Media Associated with this Article\n\nalphaXiv Toggle\n\nalphaXiv (What is alphaXiv?)\n\nLinks to Code Toggle\n\nCatalyzeX Code Finder for Papers (What is CatalyzeX?)\n\nDagsHub Toggle\n\nDagsHub (What is DagsHub?)\n\nGotitPub Toggle\n\nGotit.pub (What is GotitPub?)\n\nHuggingface Toggle\n\nHugging Face (What is Huggingface?)\n\nLinks to Code Toggle\n\nPapers with Code (What is Papers with Code?)\n\nScienceCast Toggle\n\nScienceCast (What is Scienc",

      "text": "000\n001\n002\n003\n004\n005\n006\n007\n008\n009\n010\n011\n012\n013\n014\n015\n016\n017\n018\n019\n020\n021\n022\n023\n024\n025\n026\n027\n028\n029\n030\n031\n032\n033\n034\n035\n036\n037\n038\n039\n040\n041\n042\n043\n044\n045\n046\n047\n048\n049\n050\n051\n052\n053\nUnder review as a conference paper at ICLR 2026\nFROM PSEUDO-BALANCING TO TRUE SPECIALIZA\u0002TION: MEMORY-AWARE ROUTING FOR MIXTURE-OF\u0002EXPERTS\nAnonymous authors\nPaper under double-blind review\nABSTRACT\nMixture-of-Experts(MoE) efficiently trains large models by using sparse activa\u0002tion to lower costs, selecting a few experts based on data characteristics. For\nMoE, an unbalanced expert load will lead to routing collapse or increased com\u0002putational overhead. Existing methods commonly achieve an expert-centered bal\u0002ancing strategy to solve it, prioritizing equal utilization of experts over semantic\nalignment between tokens and experts. However, this can lead to a pseudo-balance\nphenomenon: To ensure expert load balancing, the same input is randomly routed\nto different experts across training steps instead of the most matching one. It intro\u0002duces two critical issues: (1) Severe knowledge overlap among experts, resulting\nin redundant representations and inefficient parameter utilization. (2) Difficulty\nin forming and stabilizing expert specialization. These issues limit the scalability\nof models, especially large language models(LLM). To address these limitations,\nwe introduce Memory-Aware Routing (MAR), a training-phase approach that en\u0002hances existing load-balancing strategies. By equipping each expert with a mem\u0002ory buffer, our method explicitly models their long-term preferences, allowing\nhistorical experience to guide routing. This ensures that tokens are routed more\nconsistently to compatible experts, mitigating the pseudo-balance problem while\nmaintaining global load balance and fostering expert specialization. Experimental\nresults show that Memory-Aware Routing improves expert specialization by 35%\nand downstream accuracy by 2%-25%, doubles parameter efficiency, and matches\nbaseline performance with only half the experts (one-quarter of the parameters).1\n1 INTRODUCTION\nIn the domain of large-scale deep learning, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have emerged as\na powerful paradigm (Jacobs et al., 1991b; Roller et al., 2021; Zhou et al., 2022; Jordan & Jacobs,\n1994b; Lepikhin et al., 2020). By leveraging a gating mechanism to dynamically route computations\nto a subset of expert subnetworks (Fedus et al., 2022; Jiang et al., 2024), MoE successfully scales\nmodel capacity while maintaining a manageable computational cost (Dai et al., 2024; Shen et al.,\n2024; Wei et al., 2024; Jiang et al., 2024). However, in the absence of explicit constraints, MoE\ntraining often suffers from severe load imbalance, where a few experts are overly activated while\nothers remain underutilized (Lepikhin et al., 2020; Fedus et al., 2022; Zoph et al., 2022; Qiu et al.,\n2024). This imbalance leads to inefficient resource usage and hinders effective training of all ex"

      "id": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/guo2025neurips-advancing/",
      "title": "Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE | ML Anthology",
      "url": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/guo2025neurips-advancing/",
      "text": "Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE | ML Anthology\n\n# Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE\n\n## Abstract\n\nMixture-of-Experts (MoE) models enable efficient scaling of large language models (LLMs) by activating only a subset of experts per input. However, we observe that the commonly used auxiliary load balancing loss often leads to expert overlap and overly uniform routing, which hinders expert specialization and degrades overall performance during post-training. To address this, we propose a simple yet effective solution that introduces two complementary objectives: (1) an orthogonality loss to encourage experts to process distinct types of tokens, and (2) a variance loss to encourage more discriminative routing decisions. Gradient-level analysis demonstrates that these objectives are compatible with the existing auxiliary loss and contribute to optimizing the training process. Experimental results over various model architectures and across multiple benchmarks show that our method significantly enhances expert specialization. Notably, our method improves classic MoE baselines with auxiliary loss by up to 23.79\\%, while also maintaining load balancing in downstream tasks, without any architectural modifications or additional components. We will release our code to contribute to the community.\n\n## Cite\n\n### Text\n\nCopy\n\n```\nGuo et al. \"Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE.\" Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems, 2025.\n```\n\n### Markdown\n\nCopy\n\n```\n[Guo et al. \"Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE.\" Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems, 2025.](https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/guo2025neurips-advancing/)\n```\n\n### BibTeX\n\nCopy\n\n```\n@inproceedings{guo2025neurips-advancing,\n  title     = {{Advancing Expert Specialization for Better MoE}},\n  author    = {Guo, Hongcan and Lu, Haolang and Nan, Guoshun and Chu, Bolun and Zhuang, Jialin and Yang, Yuan and Che, Wenhao and Cao, Xinye and Leng, Sicong and Cui, Qimei and Jiang, Xudong},\n  booktitle = {Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems},\n  year      = {2025},\n  url       = {https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/guo2025neurips-advancing/}\n}\n```"

      "text": "Published as a conference paper at ICLR 2025\nSTATISTICAL ADVANTAGES OF PERTURBING\nCOSINE ROUTER IN MIXTURE OF EXPERTS\nHuy Nguyen† Pedram Akbarian∗,† Trang Pham∗,⋄ Trang Nguyen∗,⋄\nShuijan Zhang† Nhat Ho†\n† The University of Texas at Austin ⋄ Movian AI, Vietnam\nABSTRACT\nThe cosine router in Mixture of Experts (MoE) has recently emerged as an at\u0002tractive alternative to the conventional linear router. Indeed, the cosine router\ndemonstrates favorable performance in image and language tasks and exhibits\nbetter ability to mitigate the representation collapse issue, which often leads to\nparameter redundancy and limited representation potentials. Despite its empirical\nsuccess, a comprehensive analysis of the cosine router in MoE has been lacking.\nConsidering the least square estimation of the cosine routing MoE, we demonstrate\nthat due to the intrinsic interaction of the model parameters in the cosine router via\nsome partial differential equations, regardless of the structures of the experts, the\nestimation rates of experts and model parameters can be as slow as O(1/ logτ(n))\nwhere τ > 0 is some constant and n is the sample size. Surprisingly, these pes\u0002simistic non-polynomial convergence rates can be circumvented by the widely\nused technique in practice to stabilize the cosine router — simply adding noises\nto the ℓ\n2\n-norms in the cosine router, which we refer to as perturbed cosine router.\nUnder the strongly identifiable settings of the expert functions, we prove that the\nestimation rates for both the experts and model parameters under the perturbed\ncosine routing MoE are significantly improved to polynomial rates. Finally, we\nconduct extensive simulation studies in both synthetic and real data settings to\nempirically validate our theoretical results.\n1 INTRODUCTION\nProposed by Jacobs et al. (1991) and Jordan & Jacobs (1994), Mixture of Experts (MoE) has been\nknown as an effective statistical method to incorporate the capabilities of various specialized models\ncalled experts. Different from conventional mixture models (Lindsay, 1995) in which the mixture\nweights are scalars, the MoE rather utilizes a routing mechanism to determine a set of weights\ndepending on an input token. In particular, the router first computes the similarity scores between\neach token and experts and then assigns more weights to the more relevant experts determined\nbased on those scores. To further improve the scalability of the MoE, Shazeer et al. (2017) has\nrecently introduced a sparse variant of this model, which routes each input to only a subset of\nexperts. This sparse MoE model allows us to increase the number of learnable parameters with nearly\nconstant computational overhead. As a consequence, the sparse MoE has been leveraged in several\napplications, including large language models (Jiang et al., 2024; Puigcerver et al., 2024; Zhou et al.,\n2023b; Dai et al., 2024; Pham et al., 2024), computer vision (Riquelme et al., 2021; Liang et al.,\n2022), speech recognition (You et al"

      "text": "Low-Rank Adaptation of Time Series Foundational Models for\nOut-of-Domain Modality Forecasting\nDivij Gupta, Anubhav Bhatti, Suraj Parmar, Chen Dan, Yuwei Liu, Bingjie Shen, San Lee\nAI Engineering Team, SpassMed Inc.\nToronto, Ontario, Canada\nABSTRACT\nLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a widely used technique for fine\u0002tuning large pre-trained or foundational models across different\nmodalities and tasks. We\ndemonstrate LoRA’s fine-tuning potential for forecasting the vital\nsigns of sepsis patients in intensive care units (ICUs), emphasiz\u0002ing the models’ adaptability to previously unseen, out-of-domain\nmodalities. Integrating LoRA aims to enhance forecasting perfor\u0002mance while reducing inefficiencies associated with fine-tuning\nlarge models on limited domain-specific data.       "id": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/103027",
      "title": "NeurIPS Beyond LoRA: Exploring Efficient Fine-Tuning Techniques ...",
      "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/103027",
      "text": "NeurIPS Beyond LoRA: Exploring Efficient Fine-Tuning Techniques for Time Series Foundational Models NeurIPS 2024\n\nPoster in Workshop: Time Series in the Age of Large Models\n\n# Beyond LoRA: Exploring Efficient Fine-Tuning Techniques for Time Series Foundational Models\n\nDivij Gupta ⋅ Anubhav Bhatti ⋅ Surajsinh Parmar\n\nProject Page [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nTime Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) have recently garnered attention for their ability to model complex, large-scale time series data across domains such as retail, finance, and transportation. However, their application to sensitive, domain-specific fields like healthcare remains challenging, primarily due to the difficulty of fine-tuning these models for specialized, out-of-domain tasks with scarce publicly available datasets. In this work, we explore the use of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) techniques to address these limitations, focusing on healthcare applications, particularly ICU vitals forecasting for sepsis patients. We introduce and evaluate two selective (BitFit and LayerNorm Tuning) and two additive (VeRA and FourierFT) PEFT techniques on multiple configurations of the Chronos TSFM for forecasting vital signs of sepsis patients. Our comparative analysis demonstrates that some of these PEFT methods outperform LoRA in terms of parameter efficiency and domain adaptation, establishing state-of-the-art (SOTA) results in ICU vital forecasting tasks. Interestingly, FourierFT applied to the Chronos (Tiny) variant surpasses the SOTA model while fine-tuning only 2,400 parameters compared to the 700K parameters of the benchmark.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

      "id": "https://nips.cc/virtual/2025/loc/san-diego/poster/118294",
      "title": "NeurIPS Poster Multi-Scale Finetuning for Encoder-based Time Series Foundation Models",
      "url": "https://nips.cc/virtual/2025/loc/san-diego/poster/118294",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster Multi-Scale Finetuning for Encoder-based Time Series Foundation Models NeurIPS 2025 \n\n \n\n \n\n- San Diego\n- Mexico City\n\n \n\n \n\n \n\nPoster Fri, Dec 5, 2025 • 4:30 PM – 7:30 PM PST Exhibit Hall C,D,E #2304\n\n \n\n \n\n# Multi-Scale Finetuning for Encoder-based Time Series Foundation Models\n\n \n\nZhongzheng Qiao · Chenghao Liu · Yiming Zhang · Ming Jin · Quang Pham · Qingsong Wen · Ponnuthurai Suganthan · Xudong Jiang · Savitha Ramasamy\n\n \n\n [ Slides] [ Poster] [ OpenReview] \n\n \n\n### Abstract\n\nTime series foundation models (TSFMs) demonstrate impressive zero-shot performance for time series forecasting. However, an important yet underexplored challenge is how to effectively finetune TSFMs on specific downstream tasks. While naive finetuning can yield performance gains, we argue that it falls short of fully leveraging TSFMs' capabilities, often resulting in overfitting and suboptimal performance. Given the diverse temporal patterns across sampling scales and the inherent multi-scale forecasting capabilities of TSFMs, we adopt a causal perspective to analyze finetuning process, through which we highlight the critical importance of explicitly modeling multiple scales and reveal the shortcomings of naive approaches. Focusing on encoder-based TSFMs, we propose Multiscale finetuning (MSFT), a simple yet general framework that explicitly integrates multi-scale modeling into the finetuning process. Experimental results on three different backbones (Moirai, Moment and Units) demonstrate that TSFMs finetuned with MSFT not only outperform naive and typical parameter efficient finetuning methods but also surpass state-of-the-art deep learning methods. Codes are available at https://github.com/zqiao11/MSFT.\n\nShow more \n\n \n\n \n\n### Video\n\n \n\nChat is not available.\n\n \n\n \n\n",

      "title": "Trans-LoRA : towards data-free Transferable Parameter Efficient ...",
      "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/poster/94447",
      "publishedDate": "2024-09-26T21:56:49.000Z",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster $\\textit{Trans-LoRA}$: towards data-free Transferable Parameter Efficient Finetuning NeurIPS 2024\n\nPoster\n\n# $\\textit{Trans-LoRA}$: towards data-free Transferable Parameter Efficient Finetuning\n\nRunqian Wang ⋅ Soumya Ghosh ⋅ David Cox ⋅ Diego Antognini ⋅ Aude Oliva ⋅ Rogerio Feris ⋅ Leonid Karlinsky\n\n2024 Poster\n\n[Paper] [Poster] [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nLow-rank adapters (LoRA) and their variants are popular parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques that closely match full model fine-tune performance while requiring only a small number of additional parameters. These additional LoRA parameters are specific to the base model being adapted. When the base model needs to be deprecated and replaced with a new one, all the associated LoRA modules need to be re-trained. Such re-training requires access to the data used to train the LoRA for the original base model. This is especially problematic for commercial cloud applications where the LoRA modules and the base models are hosted by service providers who may not be allowed to host proprietary client task data. To address this challenge, we propose $\\textit{Trans-LoRA}$ --- a novel method for lossless, nearly data-free transfer of LoRAs across base models. Our approach relies on synthetic data to transfer LoRA modules. Using large language models, we design a synthetic data generator to approximate the data-generating process of the $\\textit{observed}$ task data subset. Training on the resulting synthetic dataset transfers LoRA modules to new models. We show the effectiveness of our approach using both LLama and Gemma model families. Our approach achieves lossless (mostly improved) LoRA transfer between models within and across different base model families, and even between different PEFT methods, on a wide variety of tasks.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

      "text": "FMTK: A Modular Toolkit for Composable Time Series Foundation Model Pipelines\n\n# FMTK: A Modular Toolkit for Composable Time Series Foundation Model Pipelines\n\nHetvi Shastri1 Pragya Sharma2 Walid A. Hanafy1 Mani Srivastava2† Prashant Shenoy1 1University of Massachusetts Amherst 2University of California Los Angeles\n\n###### Abstract\n\nFoundation models (FMs) have opened new avenues for machine learning applications due to their ability to adapt to new and unseen tasks with minimal or no further training. Time-series foundation models (TSFMs)—FMs trained on time-series data—have shown strong performance on classification, regression, and imputation tasks. Recent pipelines combine TSFMs with task-specific encoders, decoders, and adapters to improve performance; however, assembling such pipelines typically requires ad hoc, model-specific implementations that hinder modularity and reproducibility. We introduce FMTK, an open-source, lightweight and extensible toolkit for constructing and fine-tuning TSFM pipelines via standardized backbone and component abstractions. FMTK enables flexible composition across models and tasks, achieving correctness and performance with an average of seven lines of code. https://github.com/umassos/FMTK\n\n††footnotetext: Author holds concurrent appointments as a Professor at UCLA, and as an Amazon Scholar. This paper describes work performed at UCLA and is not associated with Amazon.\n\n### 1 Introduction\n\nTime Series Foundation Models (TSFMs), such as MOMENT Goswami2024:MOMENT, Chronos Ansari2024:Chronos, and TimesFM Das2024:TimesFM, have emerged as powerful pre-trained architectures for a variety of downstream tasks, including forecasting, classification, and imputation. While these models serve as fixed backbones trained on large-scale temporal data, effective task specialization often requires integrating additional components: input encoders to structure raw data, task-specific decoders to generate predictions, and increasingly, parameter-efficient adapters (e.g., LoRA Hu2022:LoRA) to enable lightweight fine-tuning.\n\nThis modular design space, though conceptually flexible, has resulted in a fragmented and ad hoc implementation landscape. For example, models such as PaPaGei pillai2024papagei and Mantis feofanov2025mantis require the development of extensive custom pipelines for simple comparison with state-of-the-art models. Moreover, models like Moment Goswami2024:MOMENT offer distinct modes for different tasks (e.g., forecasting versus classification), which restricts the reuse of the same powerful backbone for a diverse set of downstream tasks during runtime. As a result, three key challenges emerge. 1 First, the absence of a unifying abstraction across encoders, backbones, adapters, and decoders significantly increases the engineering burden and inhibits systematic exploration of architectural variants. 2 Second, the lack of modular encapsulation complicates the attribution.       "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2025/130467",
      "text": "NeurIPS Adaptive Refinement of Time Series Foundation Models via Pattern and Context-Awareness NeurIPS 2025 \n\n \n\n \n\n- San Diego\n- Mexico City\n\n \n\n \n\n \n\nPoster in Workshop: Recent Advances in Time Series Foundation Models: Have We Reached the ‘BERT Moment’? Sun, Dec 7, 2025 • 3:45 PM – 4:45 PM PST\n\n \n\n \n\n# Adaptive Refinement of Time Series Foundation Models via Pattern and Context-Awareness\n\n \n\nAshish Mishra · Tarun Kumar · Satish Mopur · Sergey Serebryakov · Suparna Bhattacharya · Martin Foltin · Ramanagopal Vogety · Phanidhar Koganti\n\n \n\nProject Page [OpenReview] \n\n \n\n### Abstract\n\nTime-series forecasting in domains such as ITOps and IoT faces two major challenges: data are non-stationary and multivariate, and state-of-the-art Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) rely on fixed-size windows that miss transient phenomena (e.g., spikes, drifts) and their historical context. Prior efforts address this with seasonal-trend decompositions or frequency-aware pre-training, but these require retraining and offer limited adaptability. We propose a dynamic two-stream framework that augments any pre-trained TSFM with frequency pattern awareness and contextual retrieval as the two streams. Each input window is decomposed via Fast Fourier Transforms (FFT) and Discrete Wavelet Transforms (DWT) to extract key low- and high-frequency patterns, which are fused into a TSFM through lightweight adapters and gated embedding augmentation. In parallel, frequency pattern signatures are used to retrieve semantically similar historical sequences, enriching long-range context. This approach enhances the forecasting robustness of deployed TSFMs without retraining, achieving consistent improvements over baselines on zero-shot forecasting benchmarks, particularly with abrupt data fluctuations and complex temporal dynamics.\n\nShow more\n\n \n\n \n\nChat is not available.\n\n \n\n \n\n",

    {
      "id": "https://nips.cc/virtual/2025/loc/san-diego/poster/116420",
      "title": "NeurIPS Poster Less is More: Unlocking Specialization of Time Series Foundation Models via Structured Pruning",
      "url": "https://nips.cc/virtual/2025/loc/san-diego/poster/116420",
      "publishedDate": "2025-09-19T13:23:01.000Z",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster Less is More: Unlocking Specialization of Time Series Foundation Models via Structured Pruning NeurIPS 2025 \n\n \n\n \n\n- San Diego\n- Sydney\n- Atlanta\n- Mexico City\n\n \n\n \n\n \n\nPoster Thu, Dec 4, 2025 • 4:30 PM – 7:30 PM PST\n\n \n\n \n\n# Less is More: Unlocking Specialization of Time Series Foundation Models via Structured Pruning\n\n \n\nLifan Zhao · Yanyan Shen · Zhaoyang Liu · xue wang · Jiaji Deng\n\n \n\n [OpenReview] \n\n \n\n### Abstract\n\nScaling laws motivate the development of Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) that pre-train vast parameters and achieve remarkable zero-shot forecasting performance. Surprisingly, even after fine-tuning, TSFMs cannot consistently outperform smaller, specialized models trained on full-shot downstream data. A key question is how to realize effective adaptation of TSFMs for a target forecasting task. Through empirical studies on various TSFMs, the pre-trained models often exhibit inherent sparsity and redundancy in computation, suggesting that TSFMs have learned to activate task-relevant network substructures to accommodate diverse forecasting tasks. To preserve this valuable prior knowledge, we propose a structured pruning method to regularize the subsequent fine-tuning process by focusing it on a more relevant and compact parameter space. Extensive experiments on seven TSFMs and six benchmarks demonstrate that fine-tuning a smaller, pruned TSFM significantly improves forecasting performance compared to fine-tuning original models. This ``prune-then-finetune'' paradigm often enables TSFMs to achieve state-of-the-art performance and surpass strong specialized baselines. Source code is made publicly available at \\url{https://github.com/SJTU-DMTai/Prune-then-Finetune}.\n\nShow more\n\n \n\n \n\n### Video\n\n \n\nChat is not available.\n\n \n\n \n\n",

      "id": "http://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/18c0102cb7f1a02c14f0929089b2e576-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "title": "Sparse High Rank Adapters",
      "url": "http://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/18c0102cb7f1a02c14f0929089b2e576-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "text": "Sparse High Rank Adapters\n\nBibtex Paper\n\n## Abstract\n\nLow Rank Adaptation (LoRA) has gained massive attention in the recent generative AI research. One of the main advantages of LoRA is its ability to be fused with pretrained models, adding no overhead during inference. However, from a mobile deployment standpoint, we can either avoid inference overhead in the fused mode but lose the ability to switch adapters rapidly, or suffer significant (up to 30% higher) inference latency while enabling rapid switching in the unfused mode. LoRA also exhibits concept-loss when multiple adapters are used concurrently. In this paper, we propose Sparse High Rank Adapters (SHiRA), a new paradigm which incurs no inference overhead, enables rapid switching, and significantly reduces concept-loss. Specifically, SHiRA can be trained by directly tuning only 1-2% of the base model weights while leaving others unchanged. This results in a highly sparse adapter which can be switched directly in the fused mode. We further provide theoretical and empirical insights on how high sparsity in SHiRA can aid multi-adapter fusion by reducing concept loss. Our extensive experiments on LVMs and LLMs demonstrate that finetuning only a small fraction of the parameters in the base model significantly outperforms LoRA while enabling both rapid switching and multi-adapter fusion. Finally, we provide a latency- and memory-efficient SHiRA implementation based on Parameter-Efficient Finetuning (PEFT) Library which trains at nearly the same speed as LoRA while consuming up to 16% lower peak GPU memory, thus making SHiRA easy to adopt for practical use cases. To demonstrate rapid switching benefits during inference, we show that loading SHiRA on a base model can be 5x-16x faster than LoRA fusion on a CPU.\n\n## DOI\n\n10.52202/079017-0438\n\nDo not remove: This comment is monitored to verify that the site is working properly"
    },

      "id": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2025/poster/115034",
      "title": "RefLoRA: Refactored Low-Rank Adaptation for Efficient Fine-Tuning ...",
      "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2025/poster/115034",
      "publishedDate": "2025-09-19T03:54:44.000Z",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster RefLoRA: Refactored Low-Rank Adaptation for Efficient Fine-Tuning of Large Models NeurIPS 2025\n\nPoster Fri, Dec 5, 2025 • 4:30 PM – 7:30 PM PST\n\n# RefLoRA: Refactored Low-Rank Adaptation for Efficient Fine-Tuning of Large Models\n\nYilang Zhang ⋅ Bingcong Li ⋅ Georgios Giannakis\n\nProject Page [Slides] [Poster] [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nLow-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) lowers the computational and memory overhead of fine-tuning large models by updating a low-dimensional subspace of the pre-trained weight matrix. Albeit efficient, LoRA exhibits suboptimal convergence and noticeable performance degradation, due to inconsistent and imbalanced weight updates induced by its nonunique low-rank factorizations. To overcome these limitations, this article identifies the optimal low-rank factorization per step that minimizes an upper bound on the loss. The resultant refactored low-rank adaptation (RefLoRA) method promotes a flatter loss landscape, along with consistent and balanced weight updates, thus speeding up stable convergence. Extensive experiments evaluate RefLoRA on natural language understanding, and commonsense reasoning tasks with popular large language models including DeBERTaV3, LLaMA-7B, LLaMA2-7B and LLaMA3-8B. The numerical tests corroborate that RefLoRA converges faster, outperforms various benchmarks, and enjoys negligible computational overhead compared to state-of-the-art LoRA variants.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

      "id": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/poster/94885",
      "title": "LoRA-GA: Low-Rank Adaptation with Gradient Approximation",
      "url": "https://neurips.cc/virtual/2024/poster/94885",
      "publishedDate": "2024-09-26T03:54:43.000Z",
      "text": "NeurIPS Poster LoRA-GA: Low-Rank Adaptation with Gradient Approximation NeurIPS 2024\n\nPoster\n\n# LoRA-GA: Low-Rank Adaptation with Gradient Approximation\n\nShaowen Wang ⋅ Linxi Yu ⋅ Jian Li\n\n2024 Poster\n\n[Paper] [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nFine-tuning large-scale pretrained models is prohibitively expensive in terms of computational and memory costs. LoRA, as one of the most popular Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) methods, offers a cost-effective alternative by fine-tuning an auxiliary low-rank model that has significantly fewer parameters. Although LoRA reduces the computational and memory requirements significantly at each iteration, extensive empirical evidence indicates that it converges at a considerably slower rate compared to full fine-tuning, ultimately leading to increased overall compute and often worse test performance. In our paper, we perform an in-depth investigation of the initialization method of LoRA and show that careful initialization (without any change of the architecture and the training algorithm) can significantly enhance both efficiency and performance. In particular, we introduce a novel initialization method, LoRA-GA (Low Rank Adaptation with Gradient Approximation), which aligns the gradients of low-rank matrix product with those of full fine-tuning at the first step. Our extensive experiments demonstrate that LoRA-GA achieves a convergence rate comparable to that of full fine-tuning (hence being significantly faster than vanilla LoRA as well as various recent improvements) while simultaneously attaining comparable or even better performance. For example, on the subset of the GLUE dataset with T5-Base, LoRA-GA outperforms LoRA by 5.69% on average. On larger models such as Llama 2-7B, LoRA-GA shows performance improvements of 0.34, 11.52%, and 5.05% on MTbench, GSM8k, and Human-eval, respectively. Additionally, we observe up to 2-4 times convergence speed improvement compared to vanilla LoRA, validating its effectiveness in accelerating convergence and enhancing model performance.\n\nShow more\n\n### Video\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load",

Tonello\nUniversity of Klagenfurt\n{nunzio.letizia,nicola.novello,andrea.tonello}@aau.at\nAbstract\nEstimating mutual information accurately is pivotal across diverse applications,\nfrom machine learning to communications and biology, enabling us to gain insights\ninto the inner mechanisms of complex systems.       "title": "NeurIPS  Contrastive MIM: A Contrastive Mutual Information Framework for Unified Generative and Discriminative Representation Learning",

      "text": "NeurIPS Contrastive MIM: A Contrastive Mutual Information Framework for Unified Generative and Discriminative Representation Learning NeurIPS 2025 CSP Test --\n\n- Mexico City\n- San Diego\n\nPoster in Workshop: Structured Probabilistic Inference and Generative Modeling\n\n# Contrastive MIM: A Contrastive Mutual Information Framework for Unified Generative and Discriminative Representation Learning\n\nMicha Livne\n\nProject Page [Poster] [OpenReview]\n\n### Abstract\n\nLearning representations that generalize well to unknown downstream tasks is a central challenge in representation learning. Existing approaches such as contrastive learning, self-supervised masking, and denoising auto-encoders address this challenge with varying trade-offs. In this paper, we introduce the {contrastive Mutual Information Machine} (cMIM), a probabilistic framework that augments the Mutual Information Machine (MIM) with a novel contrastive objective. While MIM maximizes mutual information between inputs and latent variables and encourages clustering of latent codes, its representations underperform on discriminative tasks compared to state-of-the-art alternatives. cMIM addresses this limitation by enforcing global discriminative structure while retaining MIM's generative strengths.We present two main contributions: (1) we propose cMIM, a contrastive extension of MIM that eliminates the need for positive data augmentation and is robust to batch size, unlike InfoNCE-based methods; (2) we introduce {informative embeddings}, a general technique for extracting enriched representations from encoder--decoder models that substantially improve discriminative performance without additional training, and which apply broadly beyond MIM.Empirical results demonstrate that cMIM consistently outperforms MIM and InfoNCE in classification and regression tasks, while preserving comparable reconstruction quality. These findings suggest that cMIM provides a unified framework for learning representations that are simultaneously effective for discriminative and generative applications.\n\nShow more\n\nChat is not available.\n\nSuccessful Page Load"

      "id": "http://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/efa9e3ed9c411aeb53d7bfe38a19d884-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "title": "Approximating mutual information of high-dimensional variables using learned representations",
      "url": "http://papers.nips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/efa9e3ed9c411aeb53d7bfe38a19d884-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "text": "Approximating mutual information of high-dimensional variables using learned representations \n\nBibtex Paper Supplemental\n\n## Abstract\n\nMutual information (MI) is a general measure of statistical dependence with widespread application across the sciences. However, estimating MI between multi-dimensional variables is challenging because the number of samples necessary to converge to an accurate estimate scales unfavorably with dimensionality. In practice, existing techniques can reliably estimate MI in up to tens of dimensions, but fail in higher dimensions, where sufficient sample sizes are infeasible. Here, we explore the idea that underlying low-dimensional structure in high-dimensional data can be exploited to faithfully approximate MI in high-dimensional settings with realistic sample sizes. We develop a method that we call latent MI (LMI) approximation, which applies a nonparametric MI estimator to low-dimensional representations learned by a simple, theoretically-motivated model architecture. Using several benchmarks, we show that unlike existing techniques, LMI can approximate MI well for variables with $> 10^3$ dimensions if their dependence structure is captured by low-dimensional representations. Finally, we showcase LMI on two open problems in biology. First, we approximate MI between protein language model (pLM) representations of interacting proteins, and find that pLMs encode non-trivial information about protein-protein interactions. Second, we quantify cell fate information contained in single-cell RNA-seq (scRNA-seq) measurements of hematopoietic stem cells, and find a sharp transition during neutrophil differentiation when fate information captured by scRNA-seq increases dramatically. An implementation of LMI is available at *latentmi.readthedocs.io.*\n\n## DOI\n\n10.52202/079017-4223\n\n \n\nDo not remove: This comment is monitored to verify that the site is working properly\n\n"

      "id": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/zhao2025neurips-partial/",
      "title": "Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions | ML Anthology",
      "url": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/zhao2025neurips-partial/",
      "text": "Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions | ML Anthology\n\n# Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions\n\nWenyuan Zhao, Adithya Balachandran, Chao Tian, Paul Pu Liang\n\n## Abstract\n\nThe study of multimodality has garnered significant interest in fields where analyzing interactions among multiple information sources can enhance predictive modeling, data fusion, and interpretability. Partial information decomposition (PID) has emerged as a useful information-theoretic framework to quantify the degree to which individual modalities independently, redundantly, or synergistically convey information about a target variable. However, existing PID methods depend on optimizing over a joint distribution constrained by estimated pairwise probability distributions, which are costly and inaccurate for continuous and high-dimensional modalities. Our first key insight is that the problem can be solved efficiently when the pairwise distributions are multivariate Gaussians, and we refer to this problem as Gaussian PID (GPID). We propose a new gradient-based algorithm that substantially enhances computational efficiency for GPID based on an alternative formulation of the underlying optimization problem. To generalize the applicability to non-Gaussian data, we learn information-preserving encoders to transform random variables of arbitrary input distributions into pairwise Gaussian random variables. Along the way, we resolved an open problem regarding the optimality of joint Gaussian solutions for GPID. Empirical validation on diverse synthetic examples demonstrates that our proposed method provides more accurate and efficient PID estimates than existing baselines. We further evaluate on a series of large-scale multimodal benchmarks to show its utility in real-world applications of quantifying PID in multimodal datasets and selecting high-performing models.\n\n## Cite\n\n### Text\n\nCopy\n\n```\nZhao et al. \"Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions.\" Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems, 2025.\n```\n\n### Markdown\n\nCopy\n\n```\n[Zhao et al. \"Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions.\" Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems, 2025.](https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/zhao2025neurips-partial/)\n```\n\n### BibTeX\n\nCopy\n\n```\n@inproceedings{zhao2025neurips-partial,\n  title     = {{Partial Information Decomposition via Normalizing Flows in Latent Gaussian Distributions}},\n  author    = {Zhao, Wenyuan and Balachandran, Adithya and Tian, Chao and Liang, Paul Pu},\n  booktitle = {Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems},\n  year      = {2025},\n  url       = {https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2025/zhao2025neurips-partial/}\n}\n```"

      "id": "https://www.arxiv.org/abs/2509.21584",
      "title": "[2509.21584] IndiSeek learns information-guided disentangled representations",
      "url": "https://www.arxiv.org/abs/2509.21584",
      "text": "[2509.21584] IndiSeek learns information-guided disentangled representations\n\n--\n\n# Statistics Machine Learning\n\narXiv:2509.21584 (stat)\n\n[Submitted on 25 Sep 2025 (v1), last revised 4 Dec 2025 (this version, v4)]\n\n# Title:IndiSeek learns information-guided disentangled representations\n\nAuthors: Yu Gui, Cong Ma, Zongming Ma\n\nView PDF HTML (experimental)\n\n> Abstract:Learning disentangled representations is a fundamental task in multi-modal learning. In modern applications such as single-cell multi-omics, both shared and modality-specific features are critical for characterizing cell states and supporting downstream analyses. Ideally, modality-specific features should be independent of shared ones while also capturing all complementary information within each modality. This tradeoff is naturally expressed through information-theoretic criteria, but mutual-information-based objectives are difficult to estimate reliably, and their variational surrogates often underperform in practice. In this paper, we introduce IndiSeek, a novel disentangled representation learning approach that addresses this challenge by combining an independence-enforcing objective with a computationally efficient reconstruction loss that bounds conditional mutual information. This formulation explicitly balances independence and completeness, enabling principled extraction of modality-specific features. We demonstrate the effectiveness of IndiSeek on synthetic simulations, a CITE-seq dataset and multiple real-world multi-modal benchmarks.\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2509.21584\n\narXiv-issued DOI via DataCite\n\n| Subjects: | Machine Learning (stat.ML); Machine Learning (cs.LG) |\n| --- | --- |\n| Cite as: | arXiv:2509.21584 [stat.ML] |\n| (or arXiv:2509.21584v4 [stat.ML] for this version) |\n\n## Submission history\n\nFrom: Yu Gui [view email] [v1] Thu, 25 Sep 2025 20:58:34 UTC (3,934 KB) [v2] Sat, 25 Oct 2025 13:49:31 UTC (3,934 KB) [v3] Tue, 25 Nov 2025 22:23:51 UTC (17,037 KB) [v4] Thu, 4 Dec 2025 17:12:37 UTC (32,405 KB)\n\nFull-text links:\n\n## Access Paper:\n\n- TeX Source\n- HTML (experimental)\n- View PDF\n\nview license\n\nCurrent browse context:\n\nstat.ML\n\n< prev| next >\n\nnew| recent| 2025-09\n\nChange to browse by:\n\ncs cs.LG stat\n\n### References & Citations\n\n- Semantic Scholar\n- Google Scholar\n\nexport BibTeX citation\n\n### Bookmark\n\nBibliographic Tools\n\n# Bibliographic and Citation Tools\n\nBibliographic Explorer Toggle\n\nBibliographic Explorer (What is the Explorer?)\n\nConnected Papers Toggle\n\nConnected Papers (What is Connected Papers?)\n\nLitmaps Toggle\n\nLitmaps (What is Litmaps?)\n\nscite.ai Toggle\n\nscite Smart Citations (What are Smart Citations?)\n\nCode, Data, Media\n\n# Code, Data and Media Associated with this Article\n\nalphaXiv Toggle\n\nalphaXiv (What is alphaXiv?)\n\nLinks to Code Toggle\n\nCatalyzeX Code Finder for Papers (What is CatalyzeX?)\n\nDagsHub Toggle\n\nDagsHub (What is DagsHub?)\n\nGotitPub Toggle\n\nGotit.pub (What is GotitPub?)\n\nHuggingface Toggle\n\nHugging Face (What is Huggingface?)\n\nLink",

Connecting Jensen-Shannon and Kullback\u0002Leibler Divergences: A New Bound for Representation Learning. NeurIPS 2025 - 39th Annual Con\u0002ference on Neural Information Processing Systems, Dec 2025, San Diego, United States. ffhal-05366881ff\nConnecting Jensen–Shannon and Kullback-Leibler\nDivergences: A New Bound for Representation\nLearning\nReuben Dorent\nInria\nreuben.dorent@inria.fr\nPolina Golland\nMIT\npolina@csail.mit.edu\nWilliam Wells III\nHarvard, MIT\nsw@bwh.harvard.edu\nAbstract\nMutual Information (MI) is a fundamental measure of statistical dependence widely\nused in representation learning. While direct optimization of MI via its definition\nas a Kullback-Leibler divergence (KLD) is often intractable, many recent methods\nhave instead maximized alternative dependence measures, most notably, the Jensen\u0002Shannon divergence (JSD) between joint and product of marginal distributions via\ndiscriminative losses. However, the connection between these surrogate objectives\nand MI remains poorly understood. In this work, we bridge this gap by deriving a\nnew, tight, and tractable lower bound on KLD as a function of JSD in the general\ncase. By specializing this bound to joint and marginal distributions, we demonstrate\nthat maximizing the JSD-based information increases a guaranteed lower bound\non mutual information. Furthermore, we revisit the practical implementation of\nJSD-based objectives and observe that minimizing the cross-entropy loss of a\nbinary classifier trained to distinguish joint from marginal pairs recovers a known\nvariational lower bound on the JSD. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our\nlower bound is tight when applied to MI estimation. We compared our lower bound\nto state-of-the-art neural estimators of variational lower bound across a range of\nestablished reference scenarios. Our lower bound estimator consistently provides a\nstable, low-variance estimate of a tight lower bound on MI. We also demonstrate its\npractical usefulness in the context of the Information Bottleneck framework. Taken\ntogether, our results provide new theoretical justi"

      "id": "https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/bdcfa850adac4a1088153881282ca972-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "title": "Mutual Information Estimation via f - f - -Divergence and Data ...",
      "url": "https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2024/hash/bdcfa850adac4a1088153881282ca972-Abstract-Conference.html",
      "publishedDate": "2024-12-16T03:54:53.000Z",
      "text": "Mutual Information Estimation via $f$-Divergence and Data Derangements\n\n## Abstract\n\nEstimating mutual information accurately is pivotal across diverse applications, from machine learning to communications and biology, enabling us to gain insights into the inner mechanisms of complex systems. Yet, dealing with high-dimensional data presents a formidable challenge, due to its size and the presence of intricate relationships. Recently proposed neural methods employing variational lower bounds on the mutual information have gained prominence. However, these approaches suffer from either high bias or high variance, as the sample size and the structure of the loss function directly influence the training process. In this paper, we propose a novel class of discriminative mutual information estimators based on the variational representation of the $f$-divergence. We investigate the impact of the permutation function used to obtain the marginal training samples and present a novel architectural solution based on derangements. The proposed estimator is flexible since it exhibits an excellent bias/variance trade-off. The comparison with state-of-the-art neural estimators, through extensive experimentation within established reference scenarios, shows that our approach offers higher accuracy and lower complexity.\n\n## DOI\n\nDo not remove: This comment is monitored to verify that the site is working properly"
